\section*{Laws of Logarithms 01\quad\small\texttt{(el3-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 01 (el3-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log_3 5 + \log_3 4 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the product law: \( \log_a x + \log_a y = \log_a(xy) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 5 + \log_3 4 = \log_3(5 \times 4)
\end{gathered}\]
The product law \( \log_a x + \log_a y = \log_a(xy) \) lets us combine a sum of logs with the same base into a single log of the product. Crucially, this is multiplication inside the log --- not \( \log_a(x+y) \), which is a common error.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify the product.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3(20)
\end{gathered}\]
5 $\times$ 4 = 20.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_3 20 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 02\quad\small\texttt{(el3-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 02 (el3-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log_2 6 + \log_2 3 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the product law \( \log_a x + \log_a y = \log_a(xy) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 6 + \log_2 3 = \log_2(6 \times 3)
\end{gathered}\]
The two logs have the same base, so the product law applies.

\textit{Step 2: Multiply the arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2(18)
\end{gathered}\]
6 $\times$ 3 = 18.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_2 18 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 03\quad\small\texttt{(el3-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 03 (el3-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \ln 7 + \ln 4 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the product law. Both terms are natural logarithms (base \( e \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 7 + \ln 4 = \ln(7 \times 4)
\end{gathered}\]
The product law works for any base, including \( e \).

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(28)
\end{gathered}\]
7 $\times$ 4 = 28.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \ln 28 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 04\quad\small\texttt{(el3-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 04 (el3-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log_5 40 - \log_5 8 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the quotient law: \( \log_a x - \log_a y = \log_a\!\left(\dfrac{x}{y}\right) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5 40 - \log_5 8 = \log_5\!\left(\frac{40}{8}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting logs is equivalent to dividing the arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify the fraction.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5(5)
\end{gathered}\]
40 $\div$ 8 = 5.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate using \( \log_a a = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5 5 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Any log of its own base equals 1.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  1 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 05\quad\small\texttt{(el3-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 05 (el3-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log_3 54 - \log_3 6 \) as a single logarithm in its simplest form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 54 - \log_3 6 = \log_3\!\left(\frac{54}{6}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting logs with the same base means dividing the arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify and evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 9 = \log_3 3^2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
54 $\div$ 6 = 9 = 3\textsuperscript{2}, so \( \log_3 9 = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 06\quad\small\texttt{(el3-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 06 (el3-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \ln 30 - \ln 5 \) as a single natural logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law to the natural logs.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 30 - \ln 5 = \ln\!\left(\frac{30}{5}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Same rule applies regardless of whether the base is \( e \) or any other number.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(6)
\end{gathered}\]
30 $\div$ 5 = 6.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \ln 6 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 07\quad\small\texttt{(el3-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 07 (el3-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( 4\log_2 3 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the power law: \( n\log_a x = \log_a(x^n) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4\log_2 3 = \log_2(3^4)
\end{gathered}\]
The coefficient becomes the exponent of the argument.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate the power.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2(81)
\end{gathered}\]
3\textsuperscript{4} = 81.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_2 81 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 08\quad\small\texttt{(el3-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 08 (el3-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \dfrac{1}{2}\log_{10} 49 \) as a single logarithm in its simplest form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{2}\log_{10} 49 = \log_{10}(49^{1/2})
\end{gathered}\]
The coefficient \( \frac{1}{2} \) becomes the exponent.

\textit{Step 2: Simplify the power --- a half-power is a square root.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(\sqrt{49}) = \log_{10} 7
\end{gathered}\]
\( 49^{1/2} = 7 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_{10} 7 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 09\quad\small\texttt{(el3-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 09 (el3-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( 3\ln 2 \) as a single natural logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3\ln 2 = \ln(2^3)
\end{gathered}\]
The coefficient 3 becomes the exponent of the argument.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate \( 2^3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(8)
\end{gathered}\]
2\textsuperscript{3} = 8.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \ln 8 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 10\quad\small\texttt{(el3-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 10 (el3-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( -2\log_5 3 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law, noting the coefficient is negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2\log_5 3 = \log_5(3^{-2})
\end{gathered}\]
A negative coefficient gives a negative exponent.

\textit{Step 2: Write with a positive exponent using index laws.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5\!\left(\frac{1}{9}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^{-2} = \dfrac{1}{3^2} = \dfrac{1}{9} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_5\!\left(\dfrac{1}{9}\right) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 11\quad\small\texttt{(el3-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 11 (el3-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( \log_4 3 + \log_4 5 - \log_4 15 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine the first two terms using the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4 3 + \log_4 5 = \log_4(15)
\end{gathered}\]
3 $\times$ 5 = 15.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the quotient law to the result and the remaining term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4(15) - \log_4(15) = \log_4\!\left(\frac{15}{15}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Now use the quotient law.

\textit{Step 3: Simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4(1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_a 1 = 0 \) for any valid base.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  0 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 12\quad\small\texttt{(el3-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 12 (el3-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( 2\log_{10} 5 + \log_{10} 4 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law to the first term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\log_{10} 5 = \log_{10}(5^2) = \log_{10}(25)
\end{gathered}\]
5\textsuperscript{2} = 25.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(25) + \log_{10}(4) = \log_{10}(25 \times 4)
\end{gathered}\]
Now add the two log terms.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(100) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
25 $\times$ 4 = 100 and \( \log_{10} 100 = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  2 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 13\quad\small\texttt{(el3-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 13 (el3-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( \log_2 12 - 2\log_2 3 + \log_2 6 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law to the middle term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\log_2 3 = \log_2(3^2) = \log_2 9
\end{gathered}\]
3\textsuperscript{2} = 9.

\textit{Step 2: Rewrite the expression.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 12 - \log_2 9 + \log_2 6
\end{gathered}\]
Now apply the quotient and product laws in order.

\textit{Step 3: Apply the quotient law to the first two terms.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\!\left(\frac{12}{9}\right) + \log_2 6 = \log_2\!\left(\frac{4}{3}\right) + \log_2 6
\end{gathered}\]
12 $\div$ 9 = 4/3.

\textit{Step 4: Apply the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\!\left(\frac{4}{3} \times 6\right) = \log_2(8) = 3
\end{gathered}\]
\( \frac{4}{3} \times 6 = 8 \) and \( 2^3 = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  3 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 14\quad\small\texttt{(el3-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 14 (el3-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( 3\ln 2 - \ln 4 \) as a single natural logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3\ln 2 = \ln(2^3) = \ln 8
\end{gathered}\]
2\textsuperscript{3} = 8.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 8 - \ln 4 = \ln\!\left(\frac{8}{4}\right) = \ln 2
\end{gathered}\]
8 $\div$ 4 = 2.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \ln 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 15\quad\small\texttt{(el3-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 15 (el3-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Express \( \log_a\!\left(\dfrac{x^3}{y}\right) \) in terms of \( \log_a x \) and \( \log_a y \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a\!\left(\frac{x^3}{y}\right) = \log_a(x^3) - \log_a(y)
\end{gathered}\]
The fraction inside the log splits into subtraction.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to \( \log_a(x^3) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3\log_a x - \log_a y
\end{gathered}\]
The power 3 comes down as a coefficient.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  3\log_a x - \log_a y \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 16\quad\small\texttt{(el3-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 16 (el3-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Express \( \ln(x^2 y) \) in terms of \( \ln x \) and \( \ln y \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(x^2 y) = \ln(x^2) + \ln(y)
\end{gathered}\]
The product inside the log splits into addition.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to \( \ln(x^2) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\ln x + \ln y
\end{gathered}\]
The power 2 comes down as a coefficient.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  2\ln x + \ln y \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 17\quad\small\texttt{(el3-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 17 (el3-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Express \( \log_{10}\!\left(\dfrac{100}{x^4}\right) \) in a simplified form using \( \log_{10} x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}\!\left(\frac{100}{x^4}\right) = \log_{10}(100) - \log_{10}(x^4)
\end{gathered}\]
The fraction splits into subtraction of logs.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate \( \log_{10}(100) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(100) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( 10^2 = 100 \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply the power law to \( \log_{10}(x^4) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2 - 4\log_{10} x
\end{gathered}\]
The power 4 comes down as a coefficient.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  2 - 4\log_{10} x \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 18\quad\small\texttt{(el3-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 18 (el3-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Express \( \ln\!\left(\dfrac{\sqrt{x}}{e^2}\right) \) in a simplified form.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{x}}{e^2}\right) = \ln(\sqrt{x}) - \ln(e^2)
\end{gathered}\]
Numerator and denominator split into subtraction.

\textit{Step 2: Write \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \) and apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{2}\ln x - \ln(e^2)
\end{gathered}\]
A square root is a half-power.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate \( \ln(e^2) \) using \( \ln(e^n) = n \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{2}\ln x - 2
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln(e^2) = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{1}{2}\ln x - 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 19\quad\small\texttt{(el3-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 19 (el3-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_3 x + \log_3 4 = \log_3 28 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the product law to combine the left-hand side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3(4x) = \log_3 28
\end{gathered}\]
Both sides are now a single log with the same base.

\textit{Step 2: Since the logs are equal, the arguments must be equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x = 28
\end{gathered}\]
If \( \log_a A = \log_a B \), then \( A = B \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 4.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 7 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 20\quad\small\texttt{(el3-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 20 (el3-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_2 x - \log_2 5 = \log_2 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the quotient law on the left-hand side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\!\left(\frac{x}{5}\right) = \log_2 6
\end{gathered}\]
Combining the two log terms using the quotient law.

\textit{Step 2: Equate the arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{x}{5} = 6
\end{gathered}\]
If the logs are equal with the same base, the arguments are equal.

\textit{Step 3: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 30
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by 5.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 30 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 21\quad\small\texttt{(el3-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 21 (el3-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 2\log_5 x = \log_5 36 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the power law to the left side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5(x^2) = \log_5 36
\end{gathered}\]
The coefficient 2 becomes the exponent of \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Equate arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 36
\end{gathered}\]
Logs with the same base and equal values have equal arguments.

\textit{Step 3: Solve, noting \( x > 0 \) since you cannot take the log of a negative number.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 6
\end{gathered}\]
\( x = \pm 6 \) from the square root, but \( x > 0 \) so \( x = 6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 6 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 22\quad\small\texttt{(el3-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 22 (el3-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_4 x + \log_4(x - 3) = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the product law to combine the left-hand side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4[x(x-3)] = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Adding logs with the same base multiplies the arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Convert the right-hand side: \( 1 = \log_4 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4[x(x-3)] = \log_4 4
\end{gathered}\]
Writing 1 as a log so both sides have the same form.

\textit{Step 3: Equate arguments and expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x-3) = 4 \Rightarrow x^2 - 3x - 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
This gives a quadratic to solve.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve, rejecting invalid solutions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-4)(x+1) = 0 \Rightarrow x = 4 \text{ or } x = -1
\end{gathered}\]
Reject \( x = -1 \) since you cannot take a log of a negative number.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 23\quad\small\texttt{(el3-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 23 (el3-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3^x = 50 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(3^x) = \log_{10} 50
\end{gathered}\]
Taking the same log of both sides preserves the equation.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to bring \( x \) down.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log_{10} 3 = \log_{10} 50
\end{gathered}\]
The power law allows us to move the unknown from the exponent.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\log_{10} 50}{\log_{10} 3} \approx 3.56
\end{gathered}\]
Using a calculator: \( \frac{1.69897}{0.47712} \approx 3.56 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 3.56 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked and the dashed line through the solution.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 24\quad\small\texttt{(el3-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 24 (el3-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 5^{2x} = 80 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(5^{2x}) = \log_{10} 80
\end{gathered}\]
Applying the same log operation to each side.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x\log_{10} 5 = \log_{10} 80
\end{gathered}\]
The exponent \( 2x \) comes down as a coefficient.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\log_{10} 80}{2\log_{10} 5} \approx \frac{1.9031}{1.3979} \approx 1.36
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( 2\log_{10} 5 \) and evaluate.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 1.36 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 25\quad\small\texttt{(el3-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 25 (el3-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 4^x = 7^{x-1} \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(4^x) = \log(7^{x-1})
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the same log to both sides.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to each side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 4 = (x-1)\log 7
\end{gathered}\]
The exponents come down as coefficients.

\textit{Step 3: Expand the right side and collect \( x \) terms.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 4 = x\log 7 - \log 7 \Rightarrow x\log 4 - x\log 7 = -\log 7
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange to get all \( x \) terms on one side.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(\log 4 - \log 7) = -\log 7 \Rightarrow x = \frac{-\log 7}{\log 4 - \log 7} = \frac{\log 7}{\log 7 - \log 4} \approx \frac{0.8451}{0.2430} \approx 3.48
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise \( x \) then divide; evaluate using a calculator.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 3.48 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 26\quad\small\texttt{(el3-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 26 (el3-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2^{x+3} = 5^x \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(2^{x+3}) = \log(5^x)
\end{gathered}\]
Same operation applied to both sides.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+3)\log 2 = x\log 5
\end{gathered}\]
Both exponents come down.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and collect \( x \) terms.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 2 + 3\log 2 = x\log 5 \Rightarrow 3\log 2 = x\log 5 - x\log 2 = x(\log 5 - \log 2)
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange so \( x \) is on one side.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{3\log 2}{\log 5 - \log 2} = \frac{3\log 2}{\log(5/2)} \approx \frac{0.9031}{0.3979} \approx 2.27
\end{gathered}\]
Using the quotient law in reverse: \( \log 5 - \log 2 = \log 2.5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.27 \)
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 27\quad\small\texttt{(el3-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 27 (el3-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Using the change of base formula \( \log_a b = \dfrac{\log_{10} b}{\log_{10} a} \), evaluate \( \log_4 20 \) to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the change of base formula with base 10.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4 20 = \frac{\log_{10} 20}{\log_{10} 4}
\end{gathered}\]
Converts the unfamiliar base 4 log into base 10 logs.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate using a calculator.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\log_{10} 20}{\log_{10} 4} = \frac{1.3010}{0.6021} \approx 2.16
\end{gathered}\]
Divide the two log values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_4 20 \approx 2.16 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 28\quad\small\texttt{(el3-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 28 (el3-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Evaluate \( \log_6 100 \) to 3 significant figures, using the change of base formula.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the change of base formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_6 100 = \frac{\log_{10} 100}{\log_{10} 6}
\end{gathered}\]
Converting to base 10.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{2}{\log_{10} 6} = \frac{2}{0.7782} \approx 2.57
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_{10} 100 = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \log_6 100 \approx 2.57 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 29\quad\small\texttt{(el3-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 29 (el3-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \log_x 64 = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write the equation in index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^3 = 64
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \). Here the base is \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \sqrt[3]{64} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
4\textsuperscript{3} = 64.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 30\quad\small\texttt{(el3-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 30 (el3-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_x 125 = \dfrac{3}{2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write in index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^{3/2} = 125
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_x 125 = \frac{3}{2} \) means \( x^{3/2} = 125 \).

\textit{Step 2: Raise both sides to the power \( \frac{2}{3} \) to isolate \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 125^{2/3}
\end{gathered}\]
The reciprocal power cancels the \( \frac{3}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = (\sqrt[3]{125})^2 = 5^2 = 25
\end{gathered}\]
Cube root of 125 is 5, and 5\textsuperscript{2} = 25.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 25 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 31\quad\small\texttt{(el3-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 31 (el3-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Write \( \log_3 6 + \log_3 7 - \log_3 14 \) as a single integer. \\[3pt]  (b) Hence, or otherwise, solve \( \log_3 6 + \log_3 7 = \log_3(2y + 14) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Apply the product law to the first two terms.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 6 + \log_3 7 = \log_3(42)
\end{gathered}\]
6 $\times$ 7 = 42.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3(42) - \log_3(14) = \log_3\!\left(\frac{42}{14}\right) = \log_3 3 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
42 $\div$ 14 = 3 and \( \log_3 3 = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Use the result from part (a): \( \log_3 6 + \log_3 7 = \log_3 42 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 42 = \log_3(2y + 14)
\end{gathered}\]
The combined left side equals \( \log_3 42 \).

\textit{Step 4: Equate arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
42 = 2y + 14 \Rightarrow 2y = 28 \Rightarrow y = 14
\end{gathered}\]
Solve the linear equation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  1 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  y = 14 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 32\quad\small\texttt{(el3-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 32 (el3-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Show that \( \log_a(x^3 y^2) = 3\log_a x + 2\log_a y \). \\[3pt]  (b) Given that \( \log_5 x = 2 \) and \( \log_5 y = 3 \), find the value of \( \log_5(x^3 y^2) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Apply the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a(x^3 y^2) = \log_a(x^3) + \log_a(y^2)
\end{gathered}\]
Product inside a log splits into addition.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to each term.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 3\log_a x + 2\log_a y
\end{gathered}\]
The powers 3 and 2 become coefficients. This completes part (a).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Substitute the given values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5(x^3 y^2) = 3\log_5 x + 2\log_5 y = 3(2) + 2(3)
\end{gathered}\]
Using the result of part (a) with \( \log_5 x = 2 \) and \( \log_5 y = 3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
= 6 + 6 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Simple arithmetic.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (b) \(\displaystyle  \log_5(x^3 y^2) = 12 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 33\quad\small\texttt{(el3-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 33 (el3-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( \log_2(3x + 5) - \log_2(x - 1) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine the left side using the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\!\left(\frac{3x+5}{x-1}\right) = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting logs is equivalent to dividing their arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Write 3 as \( \log_2(2^3) = \log_2 8 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\!\left(\frac{3x+5}{x-1}\right) = \log_2 8
\end{gathered}\]
Expressing the right side as a log to the same base.

\textit{Step 3: Equate arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3x+5}{x-1} = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Logs equal $\Rightarrow$ arguments equal.

\textit{Step 4: Solve the resulting linear equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x + 5 = 8(x-1) = 8x - 8 \Rightarrow 13 = 5x \Rightarrow x = \frac{13}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Expand, then rearrange and divide.

\textit{Step 5: Check validity: \( x - 1 = \frac{8}{5} > 0 \) \textbackslash checkmark}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{13}{5} = 2.6
\end{gathered}\]
Both logarithm arguments must be positive. Both are satisfied.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{13}{5} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 34\quad\small\texttt{(el3-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 34 (el3-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The population \( P \) of a city after \( t \) years satisfies \( P = 500\,000 \times 1.03^t \). \\[3pt]  (a) Find the population when \( t = 0 \). \\[3pt]  (b) Find the value of \( t \) when the population first exceeds \( 700\,000 \). Give your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 500\,000 \times 1.03^0 = 500\,000 \times 1 = 500\,000
\end{gathered}\]
Any number raised to the power 0 is 1.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( P = 700\,000 \) and solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
500\,000 \times 1.03^t = 700\,000 \Rightarrow 1.03^t = \frac{700\,000}{500\,000} = 1.4
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 500 000.

\textit{Step 3: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t\log_{10}(1.03) = \log_{10}(1.4)
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power law to move \( t \) from the exponent.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\log_{10}(1.4)}{\log_{10}(1.03)} \approx \frac{0.14613}{0.01284} \approx 11.4
\end{gathered}\]
Divide the two values; the population exceeds 700 000 after about 11.4 years, so at \( t = 12 \) (next complete year).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  500\,000 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 11.4 \) years
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked and the dashed line through the solution.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 35\quad\small\texttt{(el3-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 35 (el3-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{(a) Given that \( y = \log_3(x^2 + 5x) - \log_3(x) \), express \( y \) as a single logarithm. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the value of \( x \) when \( y = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \log_3\!\left(\frac{x^2+5x}{x}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting logs with the same base means dividing the arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise the numerator and cancel.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = \log_3\!\left(\frac{x(x+5)}{x}\right) = \log_3(x+5)
\end{gathered}\]
Cancel the common factor of \( x \) (valid since \( x > 0 \)).

\textit{Step 3: (b) Set \( y = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3(x+5) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( y = 2 \) into the simplified expression.

\textit{Step 4: Convert to index form and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 5 = 3^2 = 9 \Rightarrow x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( \log_3(x+5) = 2 \Leftrightarrow x+5 = 3^2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  y = \log_3(x+5) \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  x = 4 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 36\quad\small\texttt{(el3-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 36 (el3-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( \log 6 + \log 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Product rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(6 \times 5) = \log 30
\end{gathered}\]
The product law \( \log x + \log y = \log(xy) \) combines two logs of the same base into a single log of the product. Remember this works because logs convert multiplication into addition --- not \( \log(x+y) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 30
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 37\quad\small\texttt{(el3-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 37 (el3-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( \log 48 - \log 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Quotient rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log\frac{48}{6} = \log 8
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law \( \log x - \log y = \log(x/y) \) turns subtraction of two same-base logs into a single log of the quotient. Here \( 48 \div 6 = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 8
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 38\quad\small\texttt{(el3-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 38 (el3-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Simplify \( 3\log 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 2^3 = \log 8
\end{gathered}\]
The power law \( n\log x = \log(x^n) \) lets a coefficient in front of a log become an exponent inside it. Note that the coefficient becomes an exponent, not a factor --- \( 3\log 2 \ne \log 6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 8
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 39\quad\small\texttt{(el3-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 39 (el3-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log 2 + \log 3 + \log 5 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Product rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(2 \times 3 \times 5) = \log 30
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the product law twice (or extend it to three terms): a sum of logs with the same base becomes a single log of the product of the arguments.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 30
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 40\quad\small\texttt{(el3-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 40 (el3-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( 2\log 3 + \log 4 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power then product}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 9 + \log 4 = \log 36
\end{gathered}\]
First use the power law to absorb the coefficient: \( 2\log 3 = \log 3^2 = \log 9 \). Then the product law combines \( \log 9 + \log 4 = \log(9 \times 4) = \log 36 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 36
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 41\quad\small\texttt{(el3-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 41 (el3-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \log 50 - 2\log 5 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power then quotient}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 50 - \log 25 = \log\frac{50}{25} = \log 2
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power law first: \( 2\log 5 = \log 5^2 = \log 25 \). Then the quotient law turns the subtraction into a single log: \( \log 50 - \log 25 = \log(50/25) = \log 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 2
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 42\quad\small\texttt{(el3-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 42 (el3-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Express \( \log\frac{x^3}{y^2} \) in terms of \( \log x \) and \( \log y \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Expand}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log x^3 - \log y^2 = 3\log x - 2\log y
\end{gathered}\]
Use the quotient law in reverse to split the fraction into a difference of logs, then the power law brings the exponents 3 and 2 down as coefficients.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 3 log x - 2 log y
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 43\quad\small\texttt{(el3-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 43 (el3-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Express \( \log\sqrt{x} \) in terms of \( \log x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log x^{1/2} = \frac{1}{2}\log x
\end{gathered}\]
A square root is the same as a half-power: \( \sqrt{x} = x^{1/2} \). The power law then brings the \( \tfrac{1}{2} \) down as a coefficient.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (1/2) log x
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 44\quad\small\texttt{(el3-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 44 (el3-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( \log 2 = 0.301 \) and \( \log 3 = 0.477 \), find \( \log 12 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{12 = 4 x 3 = 2\textasciicircum 2 x 3}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 12 = 2\log 2 + \log 3 = 0.602 + 0.477 = 1.079
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise 12 into primes we know logs for: \( 12 = 2^2 \times 3 \). The product law gives \( \log 12 = \log 2^2 + \log 3 \), and the power law turns \( \log 2^2 \) into \( 2\log 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1.079
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 45\quad\small\texttt{(el3-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 45 (el3-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( \log 2 = 0.301 \) and \( \log 3 = 0.477 \), find \( \log 1.5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{1.5 = 3/2}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 1.5 = \log 3 - \log 2 = 0.477 - 0.301 = 0.176
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite 1.5 as the fraction \( \tfrac{3}{2} \) so the quotient law applies: \( \log(3/2) = \log 3 - \log 2 \). Then substitute the given values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 0.176
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 46\quad\small\texttt{(el3-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 46 (el3-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log x + \log(x + 3) = 1 \). (Base 10)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log[x(x+3)] = 1 \implies x(x+3) = 10
\end{gathered}\]
The product law combines \( \log x + \log(x+3) \) into one log. Since the base is 10 and \( \log_{10} 10 = 1 \), rewriting 1 as \( \log 10 \) and equating arguments gives \( x(x+3) = 10 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve quadratic}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 3x - 10 = 0 \implies (x+5)(x-2) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand and rearrange to standard form, then factorise into two brackets that multiply to give \( -10 \) and add to give \( 3 \).

\textit{Step 3: Check validity}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \text{ (reject } x = -5 \text{ as log of negative)}
\end{gathered}\]
Log arguments must be strictly positive. At \( x = -5 \), both \( \log x \) and \( \log(x+3) \) are undefined, so only \( x = 2 \) is valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 47\quad\small\texttt{(el3-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 47 (el3-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_2 x - \log_2(x - 3) = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2\frac{x}{x-3} = 2 \implies \frac{x}{x-3} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law combines the two logs into one. Rewriting 2 as \( \log_2 2^2 = \log_2 4 \) and equating arguments turns the log equation into an algebraic one.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 4x - 12 \implies 3x = 12 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( (x-3) \) to clear the fraction, then rearrange. Check the answer is valid: \( x - 3 = 1 > 0 \), so the log argument is positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 48\quad\small\texttt{(el3-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 48 (el3-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Write \( \ln a + 2\ln b - 3\ln c \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Apply laws}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln\frac{ab^2}{c^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Reverse the power law to absorb each coefficient into the argument: \( 2\ln b = \ln b^2 \) and \( 3\ln c = \ln c^3 \). Then the product law merges added logs into a single log of the product, and the quotient law turns subtraction into division.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle ln(ab^2/c^3)\)
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 49\quad\small\texttt{(el3-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 49 (el3-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show that \( \log_a b = \frac{1}{\log_b a} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Change of base}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a b = \frac{\ln b}{\ln a}; \quad \log_b a = \frac{\ln a}{\ln b}
\end{gathered}\]
The change of base formula \( \log_a b = \dfrac{\log_k b}{\log_k a} \) lets us rewrite both expressions using a common base --- natural log is the cleanest choice.

\textit{Step 2: Product}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\ln b}{\ln a} \times \frac{\ln a}{\ln b} = 1 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
The two fractions are reciprocals of each other, so their product is 1. This confirms \( \log_a b = \dfrac{1}{\log_b a} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown
\end{answerbox}

}
\end{center}
\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 50\quad\small\texttt{(el3-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 50 (el3-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2\ln x = \ln(x + 6) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Power rule on LHS}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln x^2 = \ln(x+6) \implies x^2 = x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
The power law moves the coefficient 2 up to become an exponent: \( 2\ln x = \ln x^2 \). With \( \ln \) of equal expressions on both sides, the arguments must be equal.

\textit{Step 2: Solve quadratic}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - x - 6 = 0 \implies (x-3)(x+2) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange to standard form and factorise: two numbers multiplying to \( -6 \) and summing to \( -1 \) are \( -3 \) and \( +2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Check}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3 \text{ (reject } x = -2\text{)}
\end{gathered}\]
Reject \( x = -2 \) because \( \ln x \) is undefined for negative \( x \) --- log arguments must be strictly positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 51\quad\small\texttt{(el3-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 51 (el3-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Simplify \( \frac{\log 8}{\log 2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Change of base (or use log 8 = log 2\textasciicircum 3)}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3\log 2}{\log 2} = 3
\end{gathered}\]
By the power law \( \log 8 = \log 2^3 = 3\log 2 \), so the \( \log 2 \) factors cancel. This is also the change of base formula in action: \( \dfrac{\log 8}{\log 2} = \log_2 8 = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 52\quad\small\texttt{(el3-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 52 (el3-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Simplify \( \log_2 8 + \log_2 4 - \log_2 16 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate each}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 + 2 - 4 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Each log evaluates exactly because the arguments are powers of 2: \( \log_2 8 = 3 \), \( \log_2 4 = 2 \), \( \log_2 16 = 4 \). Alternatively combine first using product and quotient laws: \( \log_2(8 \times 4 / 16) = \log_2 2 = 1 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 1
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 53\quad\small\texttt{(el3-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 53 (el3-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Express \( \log(x^2 - 1) - \log(x + 1) \) as a single simplified logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Quotient rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log\frac{x^2-1}{x+1} = \log\frac{(x-1)(x+1)}{x+1} = \log(x-1)
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law combines the two logs into a single log of the fraction. The numerator is a difference of two squares: \( x^2 - 1 = (x-1)(x+1) \), so the \( (x+1) \) factor cancels (valid provided \( x + 1 > 0 \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log(x - 1)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 54\quad\small\texttt{(el3-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 54 (el3-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_3(2x + 1) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = 3^3 = 27 \implies x = 13
\end{gathered}\]
Use the definition of a logarithm: \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \). So \( \log_3(2x+1) = 3 \) means \( 2x+1 = 3^3 = 27 \); subtract 1 and halve.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 13\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 55\quad\small\texttt{(el3-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 55 (el3-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log_5(x^2) = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 5^4 = 625 \implies x = \pm 25
\end{gathered}\]
Use the definition of a logarithm: \( \log_5(x^2) = 4 \Leftrightarrow x^2 = 5^4 = 625 \). Taking square roots gives two solutions, \( x = \pm 25 \).

\textit{Step 2: Check}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Both valid since } x^2 > 0 \text{ for both}
\end{gathered}\]
The log argument here is \( x^2 \), which is positive for both \( x = 25 \) and \( x = -25 \), so both solutions are valid. (Be careful: if the stem had been \( 2\log_5 x = 4 \), only \( x = 25 \) would survive because \( \log_5 x \) requires \( x > 0 \).)

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 25 or x = -25
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 56\quad\small\texttt{(el3-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 56 (el3-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Show that \( \log_a x^n = n\log_a x \) using the definition of logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let log\_a x = k, so a\textasciicircum k = x}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^n = (a^k)^n = a^{kn}
\end{gathered}\]
Set \( k = \log_a x \), which by the definition of a logarithm means \( a^k = x \). Raising both sides to the \( n \)th power uses the index law \( (a^k)^n = a^{kn} \).

\textit{Step 2: Take log\_a}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a x^n = kn = n\log_a x
\end{gathered}\]
Taking \( \log_a \) of both sides of \( a^{kn} = x^n \) gives \( \log_a x^n = kn \). Substitute back \( k = \log_a x \) to obtain the power law.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 57\quad\small\texttt{(el3-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 57 (el3-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_2 x + \log_2(x + 2) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2[x(x+2)] = 3 \implies x^2 + 2x = 8
\end{gathered}\]
The product law combines the left side into a single log. Then \( 3 = \log_2 2^3 = \log_2 8 \), so equating arguments gives \( x(x+2) = 8 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x - 8 = 0 \implies (x+4)(x-2) = 0 \implies x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange and factorise. Reject \( x = -4 \) since \( \log_2 x \) is undefined for negative \( x \); only \( x = 2 \) gives positive arguments in both logs.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked and the dashed line through the solution.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 58\quad\small\texttt{(el3-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 58 (el3-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( \log_a 2 = p \) and \( \log_a 5 = q \), express \( \log_a 20 \) in terms of \( p \) and \( q \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{20 = 4 x 5 = 2\textasciicircum 2 x 5}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a 20 = 2\log_a 2 + \log_a 5 = 2p + q
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise 20 in terms of the known bases: \( 20 = 2^2 \times 5 \). The product law splits the log into a sum, and the power law turns \( \log_a 2^2 \) into \( 2\log_a 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 2p + q
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 59\quad\small\texttt{(el3-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 59 (el3-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \log(x + 1) - \log x = \log 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine LHS}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log\frac{x+1}{x} = \log 3 \implies \frac{x+1}{x} = 3
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law combines the left-hand side into a single log. With logs of the same base on both sides, the arguments must be equal.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 1 = 3x \implies x = \frac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( x \) to clear the fraction, then solve linearly. Both \( x \) and \( x+1 \) are positive at \( x = \tfrac{1}{2} \), so the answer is valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1/2\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 60\quad\small\texttt{(el3-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 60 (el3-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Simplify \( \log_3 27 + \log_3 9 - \log_3 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Evaluate}
\[\begin{gathered}
3 + 2 - 1 = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Each argument is a power of 3, so the logs evaluate directly: \( \log_3 27 = 3 \), \( \log_3 9 = 2 \), \( \log_3 3 = 1 \). Or combine using product and quotient laws: \( \log_3(27 \times 9 / 3) = \log_3 81 = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 4
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 61\quad\small\texttt{(el3-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 61 (el3-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{If \( \ln a = 2 \) and \( \ln b = 5 \), find \( \ln(a^2 b) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Expand}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\ln a + \ln b = 4 + 5 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Use the product law to split \( \ln(a^2 b) \) into \( \ln a^2 + \ln b \), then the power law brings the exponent down: \( \ln a^2 = 2\ln a \). Substitute the given values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 9
\end{answerbox}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 62\quad\small\texttt{(el3-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 62 (el3-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2^{x+3} = 5^{x-1} \). Give answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+3)\ln 2 = (x-1)\ln 5
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides and apply the power law to bring each exponent down as a coefficient. This is the standard trick for getting \( x \) out of an exponent.

\textit{Step 2: Expand}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\ln 2 + 3\ln 2 = x\ln 5 - \ln 5
\end{gathered}\]
Distribute the brackets on both sides so the \( x \) terms are separated from the constants.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for x}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(\ln 2 - \ln 5) = -\ln 5 - 3\ln 2 \implies x = \frac{\ln 5 + 3\ln 2}{\ln 5 - \ln 2} = \frac{3.688}{0.916} = 4.03
\end{gathered}\]
Gather \( x \) terms on one side, factor out \( x \), then divide. Flipping the sign of numerator and denominator gives the tidier form with positive values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4.03\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 63\quad\small\texttt{(el3-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 63 (el3-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Prove that \( \log_a b \times \log_b c = \log_a c \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Change of base}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\ln b}{\ln a} \times \frac{\ln c}{\ln b} = \frac{\ln c}{\ln a} = \log_a c
\end{gathered}\]
Rewrite each log using the change of base formula with natural log. The \( \ln b \) appears in the numerator of one fraction and the denominator of the other, so it cancels --- leaving \( \dfrac{\ln c}{\ln a} = \log_a c \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Proven
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 64\quad\small\texttt{(el3-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 64 (el3-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Express \( \log\left(\frac{100x^3}{y}\right) \) in terms of \( \log x \) and \( \log y \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Expand}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 100 + 3\log x - \log y = 2 + 3\log x - \log y
\end{gathered}\]
Split the fraction using the quotient law, then split the product in the numerator using the product law, and finally bring exponents down using the power law. Since the base is 10, \( \log 100 = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 2 + 3 log x - log y
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 65\quad\small\texttt{(el3-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 65 (el3-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_4 x + \log_4(x - 6) = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_4[x(x-6)] = 2 \implies x^2 - 6x = 16
\end{gathered}\]
The product law merges the two logs into one. Since \( 2 = \log_4 4^2 = \log_4 16 \), equating arguments gives \( x(x-6) = 16 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 6x - 16 = 0 \implies (x-8)(x+2) = 0 \implies x = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise the quadratic and reject \( x = -2 \) since it makes both \( \log_4 x \) and \( \log_4(x-6) \) undefined. At \( x = 8 \), both arguments are positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 8\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 66\quad\small\texttt{(el3-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 66 (el3-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Write \( \frac{1}{2}\log 9 + \frac{1}{3}\log 8 \) as a single logarithm.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Power rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 9^{1/2} + \log 8^{1/3} = \log 3 + \log 2 = \log 6
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power law in reverse to absorb the fractional coefficients into the arguments: \( \tfrac{1}{2}\log 9 = \log 9^{1/2} = \log 3 \) and \( \tfrac{1}{3}\log 8 = \log 8^{1/3} = \log 2 \). Then the product law combines them: \( \log 3 + \log 2 = \log 6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad log 6
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 67\quad\small\texttt{(el3-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 67 (el3-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Given \( \log_a 2 = x \), express \( \log_a 32 \) in terms of \( x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{32 = 2\textasciicircum 5}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_a 32 = 5\log_a 2 = 5x
\end{gathered}\]
Spot that \( 32 = 2^5 \), then the power law gives \( \log_a 2^5 = 5\log_a 2 \). Substitute \( \log_a 2 = x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 5x
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 68\quad\small\texttt{(el3-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 68 (el3-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(3x) - \ln(x - 1) = \ln 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine LHS}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln\frac{3x}{x-1} = \ln 5 \implies \frac{3x}{x-1} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law combines the two natural logs into one. With \( \ln \) of equal expressions on both sides, the arguments must be equal.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = 5x - 5 \implies 2x = 5 \implies x = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( (x-1) \), rearrange and solve. Check validity: at \( x = 2.5 \) both \( 3x \) and \( x-1 = 1.5 \) are positive, so the answer is valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2.5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 69\quad\small\texttt{(el3-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 69 (el3-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Show that \( \log_2 3 \times \log_3 4 \times \log_4 8 = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Chain rule}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 3 \times \log_3 4 \times \log_4 8 = \log_2 8 = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the chain identity \( \log_a b \times \log_b c = \log_a c \) twice --- derived from change of base, where intermediate log terms cancel. The chain telescopes from base 2 to argument 8, and \( \log_2 8 = 3 \) because \( 2^3 = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Shown: equals 3
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}

\small The logarithmic law shown on \(y=\log_b x\), with the key points marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Laws of Logarithms 70\quad\small\texttt{(el3-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Laws of Logarithms 70 (el3-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( (\log x)^2 - 5\log x + 6 = 0 \). (Base 10)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = log x}
\[\begin{gathered}
u^2 - 5u + 6 = 0 \implies (u-2)(u-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( u = \log x \) to turn the equation into a standard quadratic. This is the "hidden quadratic" trick --- the equation is quadratic in \( \log x \), not in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log x = 2 \implies x = 100; \quad \log x = 3 \implies x = 1000
\end{gathered}\]
Back-substitute each \( u \) value and convert from log to index form: \( \log_{10} x = u \Leftrightarrow x = 10^u \). Both solutions are positive, so both are valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 100 or x = 1000
\end{answerbox}
